[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft25</sty> This is the view taken by most—<sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>,
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty>. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> has been led astray by a supposed parallelism between this and the next sentence; and he regards God to be meant. <sty c="000000" i="1">Scott</sty> has taken it in both views, but this is not to explain the passage. Indeed the very argument here used renders it necessary that Abraham, or Isaac, or Jacob, should be intended. Taking God to be meant by “father,” some have been led to think that it is the language of the Jews who married strange wives, in their own defense, “Have we not all, Gentiles as well as Jews, one Father? and has not the same God created me?” This might do well until we come to the conclusion of the verse, where the violation of the <sty c="000000" i="1">covenant</sty> of the fathers is mentioned.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft26</sty> The word <sty c="008080" i="0">dgb</sty>, as a noun, which is its root, means a robe, a cloak, or a
[BODY] covering; when used as a verb, it signifies to cover or cloak things over, and so to act falsely, hypocritically, or treacherously. <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>’ definition is, to act perfidiously, to prevaricate, to deceive. It is rendered here improperly by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">egkateli>pete</sty>—ye have forsaken.” It is here in the future tense, and may be rendered as though it were in the subjunctive mood,—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Why should we act perfidiously, each one with his brother,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By violating the covenant of our fathers?</sty>

--- PAGE 174 ---

[BODY] “Violating” is <sty c="008080" i="0">llj</sty>, which means to perforate, to pierce, and to break in, so as to violate a holy place, and hence to profane; and so it is rendered by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>—<sty c="0000d4" i="0">tou bebhlwsai</sty>. To profane one’s word in <sty c="800000" i="0"><043002></sty>Numbers 30:2, is to break it; and to profane a covenant in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><195520></sty>Psalm 55:20, is to break it; and so it is rendered in both these
[BODY] places in our version. To<sty c="000000" i="1"> break</sty> a covenant is a metaphor not very unlike that of <sty c="000000" i="1">piercing</sty> or <sty c="000000" i="1">perforating</sty> it. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> says that it refers to the ancient mode of cancelling bonds, which was done by striking a nail through them. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><510214></sty>Colossians 2:14. “Hence the word,” he adds, “signifies to <sty c="000000" i="1">make void</sty>.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft27</sty> It is <sty c="008080" i="0">hdgb</sty> in the feminine gender, because by Judah is meant the tribe
[BODY] or the family; so Ephraim is often regarded. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><280418></sty>Hosea 4:18; 5:9; 9:13. We find here Judah, Israel, and Jerusalem mentioned; and probably because the purpose was to include the whole of the people, as some of Israel or of the ten tribes were among them.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft28</sty> This last clause has been variously explained: “whom,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.</sty>, Judah, “he
[BODY] loved,” or, “which,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.,</sty> holiness, “he loved,” or, “which he,” Judah “had loved.” The last seems the most natural construction according to the tenor of the passage, if <sty c="008080" i="0">rça</sty> be a relative; for Judah is the subject in the sentence. Judah did in former times love and delight in that separating which God had made and appointed between his people and the heathen world. To say that God loved it seems an odd idea; but to say that Judah delighted in it was much to the purpose, and added it for the sake of enhancing the guilt of that generation.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> gives this version,— <sty c="000080" i="0">For he profanes Judah, the holiness of Jehovah, Who loves and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">marries a foreign wife.</sty>

[BODY] But more suitable to the genius of the language would be this,— <sty c="000080" i="0">For profaned has Judah the holiness of Jehovah, Because he has</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">loved and married The daughter of a strange God.</sty>

[BODY] The word <sty c="008080" i="0">rça</sty> is often a conjunction as well as a relative; <sty c="000000" i="1">because</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">for</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">inasmuch</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">as</sty>. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><013413></sty>Genesis 34:13; <sty c="800000" i="0"><053016></sty>Deuteronomy 30:16;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><091515></sty>1 Samuel 15:15.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft29</sty> “The holiness of Jehovah,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.,</sty> the holiness required and enjoined by
[BODY] Jehovah. Most agree that what is meant is the separation from any

--- PAGE 175 ---

[BODY] alliance with heathens. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><050703></sty>Deuteronomy 7:3. Ezra mentions Israel as “the holy seed,” <sty c="800000" i="0"><150902></sty>Ezra 9:2. See also <sty c="800000" i="0"><240203></sty>Jeremiah 2:3. <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, after <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty>, takes this view, and so do <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry,</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Scott</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft30</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Him that teacheth and him that answereth.—</sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Newcome</sty><sty c="000080" i="1">.</sty>

[INDENT] <sty c="000000" i="1">or</sty>,

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft31</sty> It is not easy to give a version of this verse. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders the
[BODY] first line thus—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And this ye have done the second time.</sty>

[BODY] The reference is, he says, to the repetition of the evil which had been corrected under Ezra 9 and 10. This seems probable; but we may view this “second,” or again, with regard to the previous denunciations. What are regarded as verbs in the infinitive mood are in my view participial nouns; the last, <sty c="008080" i="0">tjql</sty>, is evidently so. Then the literal rendering would be this— <sty c="000080" i="0">And this again ye do— Covering with tears the altar, Weeping and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">groaning, Because</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> there </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">is no more turning to the offering, Or the</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">receiving of what is acceptable from your hand.</sty>

[BODY] That <sty c="008080" i="0">ˆyam</sty> is to be rendered “because not,” or, “inasmuch as not,” is evident from other places. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><241006></sty>Jeremiah 10:6; 19:11. “Turning” signifies having a regard to. “What is acceptable,” <sty c="008080" i="0">ˆwxr</sty>, is rendered “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">dekton</sty>—acceptable,” in the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>; “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">eJudoki>an</sty>—good-will,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Aq.; </sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">to< eudokhme>non</sty>—what is approved,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Sym</sty>.; “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">te>laion</sty>— perfect,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>.

--- PAGE 176 ---

[BODY] The difference between <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> and most expositors after him, as well as before him, is, that he regarded the lamentation to have been by the priests and people, and they by the repudiated wives. The cause of the weeping, as stated here, was the rejection of the offerings, as declared by the Prophet; and this seems enough to confirm <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin’s </sty>view.
[BODY] The priests and people had been denounced for their wickedness, especially for marrying strange wives. After this denunciation they “again” went to the altar and wept because God would not receive their sacrifices; and they did this without amending their ways. Then in the next verse the Prophet explains why God would not receive their offerings.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft32</sty> Or, “a witness has Jehovah been between thee and thy wife.” But
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, and also <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius</sty>, refer this to God’s testimony in the first institution of marriage, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><010224></sty>Genesis 2:24. More suitabele to the context no doubt is to consider God as a witness to the marriage contract; and this is the view taken by <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Scott</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft33</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Koinwno>v</sty>—partner,” by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>; “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">oJmo>sarkov</sty>—of the same
[BODY] flesh,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty>; “particeps—partner,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>; “companion,” in our version, and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. [FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft34</sty> The position of the words shows that it is a question, for there is no

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft37</sty> The interpretation given of the first clause of this verse is according to
[BODY] the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty>, and has been adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, and others. Our version is derived from <sty c="000000" i="1">Jun</sty>. and <sty c="000000" i="1">Trem.,</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>, and has been followed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Lowth</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Scott</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Adam Clarke</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. The second clause has been variously interpreted both by the ancients and the moderns. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> make “violence,” or wrong, the nominative to “cover,” and the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> the accusative. “Iniquity shall cover his garment,” is the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>. “For he covers violence <sty c="000000" i="1">as </sty>with his garments,” has been the version of others; which corresponds with the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty>, as the former does with the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>.
[CENTERED] The most natural construction of the first part is no doubt what our version exhibits; the meaning of the second is less obvious: but they seem connected. What seems to be said is,—that God hates the divorcer, and him also who maltreats his wife without divorcing her. Then we may give this literal rendering,— <sty c="000080" i="0">For he hates the divorcer, (or him who puts away,) Saith Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">the God of Israel; And the coverer of outrage on his own garment,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">Saith Jehovah of hosts.</sty>

[CENTERED] To speak of God here in the third person is in accordance with the preceding verses. “His own garment,” according to <sty c="000000" i="1">Venema</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, is a figurative designation of a wife. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><080309></sty>Ruth 3:9; <sty c="800000" i="0"><261608></sty>Ezekiel 16:8.
[BODY] The condemning of divorce is more suitable to this place, than any reference to its permission; because in the previous part the allusion is evidently made to the first institution of marriage, and not to any posterior modification.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft38</sty> There is a stronger word employed by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>—
[BODY] “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">parwxu>namen</sty>—have we irritated, <sty c="000000" i="1">or</sty>, provoked.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft39</sty> Some have contended that from the order in which the words occur, the
[BODY] rendering ought to be as follows— <sty c="000080" i="0">Whosoever makes evil good in the eyes of Jehovah, Even in them</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">he delights. (See </sty><sty c="800000" i="0"><230520></sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Isaiah 5:20.)</sty>

[BODY] The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> favor this version, as the word for “good,” <sty c="0000d4" i="0">kalon</sty>, is in the accusative case. But the usual rendering is the best—

--- PAGE 178 ---

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Every doer of evil </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">is </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">good (approved) in the eyes of Jehovah, And</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">in them he delights.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius</sty> observes on these words—“None are so impiously bold as actually to express such words, but Scripture is wont to ascribe to the wicked such expressions as are suitable to their character.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[BODY] The remark of <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> and of others on the <sty c="008080" i="0">h</sty> before “Lord” as being emphatic, is not well founded. It is owing to the relative “whom” which follows, as it is in our language.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft44</sty> “A phrase nowhere else in Scripture.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Secker</sty>.
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft45</sty> Owing to this repetition, some of the fathers, <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Eusebius</sty>, and

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft46</sty> For “who will endure,” the <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate</sty>, after <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, has, “quis poterit

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft47</sty> The version of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> is “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">wJv pur cwneuthri>ou kai wJv poia</sty>
[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="0">pluno></sty><sty c="0000d4" i="0">ntwn </sty>—as the fire of the crucible (or, of the furnace) and as the herb of the washers.” The word, <sty c="008080" i="0">ãrxm</sty>, may be either a participle or a noun—the refiner or the place or instrument of refining. See
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><201703></sty>Proverbs 17:3; 27:21. The latter sense is most suitable to this
[BODY] place. “Herb” is rendered “smegma—soap,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Picator</sty>,—“Lanariacudwort,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>,—and “alkaline salt,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Michaelis</sty>. This water became impregnated with strong lixivial salt, “proper for taking,” he says, “stains and impurities out of wool or cloth.” It is not supposed that what we call “soap” was known to the Jews.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft48</sty> “Fundet <sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty> conflabit,” so he renders <sty c="008080" i="0">qqz</sty>, which signifies to fuse, as
[BODY] given by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ceei</sty>. It properly means to strain off or separate, that is, wine from its lees, as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><232506></sty>Isaiah 25:6; or, as here, the pure metal from its dross. It intimates such a process as is successful in separating the gold and silver from the base matter that may adhere to them. So that the “expurgans—cleansing” of <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>, or the “defaecans—defecating or fining from dregs,” of <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius</sty>, very nearly expresses the idea. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> translate this verb “refine,” as they do another verb, or rather participle, at the beginning of the verse. “He will strain them, (<sty c="000000" i="1">colabit</sty>,)” is the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>. Our version has “purge,” but “cleanse” is better. “Defacate” comes nearest to the original word. I would offer the following version— <sty c="000080" i="0">3. And sit will the fuser and purifier of silver; And he will purify</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">the sons of Levi, And draw off their dross as that of silver and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">gold; And they shall be to Jehovah The offerers of oblation in</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">righteousness.</sty>

[BODY] The paraphrase of <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> is substantially faithful—
[BODY] As the gold-finer (or goldsmith—<sty c="000000" i="1">aurifaber</sty>) and the purifier of silver sits, so he will purify the posterity of Levi, and will clarify (<sty c="000000" i="1">eliquabit</sty>) them as gold and silver, that they may rightly offer gifts to Jehovah.— <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft51</sty> The words may be so rendered as to allow the copulative <sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty> its ordinary
[BODY] meaning. The verse contains two announcements bearing on the subject in hand,— <sty c="000080" i="0">For I </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">am </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Jehovah, I have not changed; And ye </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">are</sty><sty c="000080" i="0"> the house of</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">Jacob, ye have not been consumed.</sty>

[CENTERED] This, I conceive, is the natural rendering of the original. God was not changed, because he was Jehovah; and they were not consumed, because they were the house of Jacob, a people in covenant with God.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft52</sty> The words are singular, “days,” being preceded by two prepositions, <sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft56</sty> The literal rendering is—

[BODY] That is—“Let what you rob me of, the prey, or plunder, be in my house.” The word is <sty c="008080" i="0">ãrf</sty>, properly prey, or plunder, and so rendered by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">diarpagh<</sty>—plunder.” It was the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> that gave a wrong meaning to the word, which most have followed.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft58</sty> <sty c="008080" i="0">yd</sty> not only means sufficiency, but also what is necessary to suffice,
[BODY] demand, requirement, as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><032526></sty>Leviticus 25:26, <sty c="008080" i="0">wtlag ydk</sty>, according to the demand of his redemption, or what was necessary or sufficient for his redemption. [FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft59</sty> There is no necessity for giving to <sty c="008080" i="0">lkç</sty> here any other than its
[BODY] ordinary meaning of bereaving or depriving. The reference is to depredators who bereaved or stripped the vine of its fruit—an evil common in a confused and disordered state of things.
[BODY] The word <sty c="008080" i="0">µkl</sty>, “on your account,” is repeated in this verse three times; and it has no doubt an emphatic meaning. What is intimate evidently is, that the evils promised here to be removed were on their account, <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.,</sty> for their sins. [FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft62</sty> The verse is differently arranged in our version, and by most

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft63</sty> “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">Iketai</sty>—supplicants,” by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint,</sty>—“tristes—sad,” by
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>,—“with a depressed spirit,” by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty>,—“mournfully,” in our version, and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. The first meaning of the word is “black” or “dark;” but it is used to express grief, sorrow, or mourning. It is rendered “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">skuqrwpazwn</sty>—being gloomy or sorrowful,” by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><193806></sty>Psalm 38:6; 42:9; 43:2. It is here used adverbially, and may be rendered either sorrowfully or mournfully. “The walking mournfully has reference to those going about in sackcloth and ashes, pretending to sorrow on account of their sins.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>.
[CENTERED] “They walked mournfully before God,” observes <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry</sty>. “Whereas God had required them to serve him with gladness, and to walk cheerfully before him. They, by their own superstitions, made the

--- PAGE 184 ---

[BODY] service of God a task and drudgery to themselves, and then complained of it as a hard service. The yoke of Christ is easy, it is the yoke of Antichrist that is heavy.”—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft64</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Leigh</sty> says, that the verb, from which the word rendered here “proud”

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft65</sty> Or, “talked together:” the verb is in Niphal, as we find it in verse 13.
[BODY] The good as well as the wicked talked together, mutually conversed, or talked often. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> renders it, “They multiplied speech;” our version introduces “often.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> give the simple word, “spake;” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> has “conversed.” If the verb in Niphal has a frequentative meaning, and not a reciprocal, our version is right, “spake often.” Then it should be so rendered in verse 13. It is to be observed that what the ungodly often spoke or said, is mentioned, but not the frequent or the mutual converse of the godly. <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty> imagines it to have been a defense of God’s dealings with them.
[BODY] The words which follow, “Every one to his neighbor,” seem to favor the opinion that speaking “often” is the real meaning of the verb here used; for the fact of speaking “together” is conveyed in these words: and yet speaking “together” is more suitable in the thirteenth verse.— <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft66</sty> In the “book of remembrance” we have an allusion to the records kept

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft67</sty> This latter meaning is the true one. The word never means what is
[BODY] understood by “thinking on” a thing; but to count, to reckon, and hence to contrive, to plan, to devise, and hence also to make an account of, to value, to regard. To make an account of and thus to regard and reverence, is its meaning here. The whole verse may be thus rendered,— <sty c="000080" i="0">16. Then spake they often who feared Jehovah, Every one to his</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">neighbor; And hearken did Jehovah and hear; And there was</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">written a book of remembrance before him, For those who feared</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">Jehovah, Yea, for those who regarded his name.</sty>

[CENTERED] The last two lines describe the same persons,—they feared God and valued and regarded his name or his authority.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft68</sty> Such is the arrangement of the sentence as given in the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, the
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty>, and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, and most interpreters. “The peculiar treasure” is connected with “they shall be to me,” and not with the verb “make,” as in our version, which is that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Jun</sty>. and <sty c="000000" i="1">Trem</sty>. The intervening clause, “In the day,” etc., may be rendered in a way different. The verb “to make” means something to appoint, to ordain, to constitute. The following version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> is no doubt the correct one—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">They shall be unto me, saith Jehovah of hosts,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In the day which I shall appoint, a peculiar treasure.</sty>

[BODY] The “day” is again mentioned in the next chapter, verse 3, and the same words come after it, which ought to be rendered in the same way. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>’s version is materially the same.
[BODY] The word rendered “jewels” in our version, is everywhere also rendered a peculiar treasure, or a special property. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><021905></sty>Exodus 19:5; <sty c="800000" i="0"><050706></sty>Deuteronomy 7:6. The common rendering of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> is either <sty c="0000d4" i="0">peripoihsin </sty>—a purchased acquisition, as here, or <sty c="0000d4" i="0">periousion </sty>—peculiar, special, as in <sty c="800000" i="0"><021905></sty>Exodus 19:5. The latter is the word used here by <sty c="000000" i="1">Symmachus</sty>.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft69</sty> There is something more in the verb here used than the idea of

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft71</sty> Exceedingly forcible are the words of this verse—

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft72</sty> There is something incongruous in the expression, “the Sun of
[BODY] righteousness.” Hence some have considered that <sty c="008080" i="0">hqdx</sty> means here benignity or beneficence. “Righteousness,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Leigh</sty>, “in a special sense, in the Hebrew and the Oriental tongues, signifieth <sty c="000000" i="1">beneficence </sty>or <sty c="000000" i="1">bounty</sty>;” and he refers to <sty c="000000" i="1">Mede</sty> on <sty c="800000" i="0"><19B206></sty>Psalm 112:6. It is evidently added as descriptive of what the sun is, and used as the case often is in Hebrew, instead of an adjective. Now a righteous sun would not be proper, but a benignant or a beneficient sun would convey a suitable idea. The real meaning would then be conveyed by such a vision as the following,— <sty c="000080" i="0">But arise for you, who fear my name, Shall a beneficient sun, With</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">healing in its beams, And ye shall go forth and leap Like calves</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">freed from the stall.</sty>

[BODY] “Understand,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, “by righteousness either benignity and beneficence, or truth, or complete constancy, or the manifold righteousness of God, which shone in him, or incontaminated uprightness and rectitude which appeared in him both as God and man, or as Mediator, which so shines, that he diffuses it to all the faithful in the gifts of justification and sanctification.”
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>’s exposition is, that Christ is called the Sun of righteousness, because he determines all things justly, and reveals, discovers what is good and what is evil, what is virtuous and what is vicious.

--- PAGE 187 ---

[BODY] The pronoun affixed to “wings,” or beams, or rays, is feminine, which shows the gender of “sun,” <sty c="008080" i="0">çmç</sty>; but “its” is the most appropriate rendering. He or she is everything in Hebrew, and it is in so Welsh.— <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft73</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>’s version of the last line is as follows—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And ye shall go forth and thrive as bullocks of the stall.</sty>

[INDENT] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>’s is—
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And ye shall go forth and leap as calves of the stall.</sty>

[BODY] The latter part is rendered by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> “Ye shall leap (or frisk— <sty c="0000d4" i="0">skirth>sete</sty>) like calves let loose from bonds.” This conveys the idea, for <sty c="008080" i="0">hçp</sty>, means first to spread, to be diffused, and then to range at large, to leap, to frisk. The context favors this view: they would go forth ,that is, from confinement to the fields, and leap like calves of the stall, or from the stall, which are tied up during the night but are let loose when the sun arises, and allowed to range at large in the field.
[BODY] To apply this as a prophecy to the escape of the Christians from Jerusalem when destroyed by the Romans, has nothing in the context to justify it, but everything to the contrary. The effect here produced is ascribed to the influence of the Sun of righteousness, and it is exhilarating and joyful, and followed, as it appears from the next verse, by the subjugation of the wicked. <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>’s view is consistent with the whole tenor of the passage.—<sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
